Tools","url":"/security-labs/blog/category/integrations-tools","locale":"en-us","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","updated_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","created_at":"2026-08-12T12:28:20.823Z","updated_at":"2026-08-12T12:28:20.823Z","ACL":{},"_version":1,"coding_language":false,"tags":[],"_in_progress":false,"publish_details":{"time":"2026-08-26T17:21:49.330Z","user":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","environment":"blt835e2c3b7e9eb7fb","locale":"en-us"}}],"created_at":"2026-08-13T08:19:37.119Z","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","date":"2026-04-25T00:00:00.000Z","main_content":{"markdown_l10n":"As AI coding assistants become standard tools in engineering workflows, security teams face a new challenge: how do you maintain visibility into what an AI agent is doing (and why) across your organization? When those agents can execute shell commands, read files, call APIs, and interact with internal systems via MCP connectors, you need real-time observability to support threat detection, incident response, and compliance.\n\nThis post walks through how Elastic's InfoSec team built a monitoring pipeline for [Claude Code](https://code.claude.com/) and [Claude Cowork](https://claude.com/docs/cowork) using their native [OpenTelemetry (OTel)](https://opentelemetry.io/) export capabilities and Elastic's own OTel ingestion infrastructure. We cover the telemetry schema, the gateway deployment, custom Elasticsearch mappings and ingest pipelines, managed configuration delivery, and the security use cases enabled by this data.\n\n## Why Elastic's InfoSec team monitors AI agents\n\nAt Elastic, we practice what we call \"Customer Zero.\" The InfoSec team is the first and most demanding user of Elastic's products, always running the newest versions in production. Our goal is to use our own products to improve our security posture whenever we can.\n\nClaude Code and Cowork are now in active use across Elastic's engineering organization. Claude Code runs locally on developer machines as a CLI-based AI coding assistant. Cowork is part of the Claude Desktop app and also runs locally. It can read files, execute code in a sandbox, search the web, and interact with connected services like Slack, GitHub, Jira, and Google Calendar through MCP connectors. Both tools support connecting to internal systems, which means they operate in a trust boundary that security teams need to monitor.\n\n## What Claude Code and Cowork export via OpenTelemetry\n\n![](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt36c8796faec1efd7/6a7d7e0a3ce8e24898cf25f0/image2.png)\n\nBoth products export telemetry through standard OpenTelemetry protocols, emitting the same five event types:\n\n* `api_request` — model, cost, token counts, latency  \n* `tool_result` — tool name, MCP server and tool name, success/failure, duration  \n* `tool_decision` — auto-approved vs user-approved  \n* `user_prompt` — what the user asked the agent to do  \n* `api_error` — error message, status code\n\nEvery event includes user identity (`user.email`, `organization.id`), session context (`session.id`, `prompt.id`, `event.sequence`), and cost/token fields on API request events. Claude Code telemetry is opt-in and redacts prompts and tool arguments by default; enable them with `OTEL_LOG_USER_PROMPTS=1` and `OTEL_LOG_TOOL_DETAILS=1`. Cowork is configured centrally in the Anthropic admin portal and includes full details automatically.\n\nFor the full telemetry schema, see the [Claude Code Monitoring documentation](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/monitoring-usage) and the [Claude Cowork Monitoring documentation](https://claude.com/docs/cowork/monitoring).\n\n## Architecture: Getting the data to Elasticsearch\n\nThere are two ways to get Claude Code and Cowork OTel data into Elasticsearch. We deployed the self-managed gateway approach first, but Elastic Cloud users have a simpler option.\n\n### Option 1: EDOT OTel Gateway (self-managed)\n\nThis is the approach we used internally. Since Elastic's InfoSec team runs self-managed ECK (Elastic Cloud on Kubernetes) clusters, we deployed an [Elastic Distribution of the OpenTelemetry Collector (EDOT)](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/edot-collector) as a gateway. Both Claude Code and Cowork run locally on user machines and send OTLP/HTTP to the gateway, which authenticates the request and writes to Elasticsearch.\n\n![](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt96dd4e11dc6411e2/6a7d7e0de3a2195d4099c693/image4.png)\n\nWe used the [opentelemetry-collector Helm chart](https://github.com/open-telemetry/opentelemetry-helm-charts) with the EDOT collector image (`docker.elastic.co/elastic-agent/elastic-otel-collector`). The EDOT image provides native Elastic data stream routing, which is important for getting logs into the right data streams without extra configuration.\n\nThe gateway runs in deployment mode and uses bearer token authentication via the [`bearertokenauth` extension](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/edot-collector/config/authentication-methods).\n\nHere is the core collector configuration:\n\n```yaml\nconfig:\n  extensions:\n    bearertokenauth:\n      scheme: \"Bearer\"\n      token: \"${env:OTEL_GATEWAY_TOKEN}\"\n  receivers:\n    otlp:\n      protocols:\n        http:\n          endpoint: \"0.0.0.0:4318\"\n          auth:\n            authenticator: bearertokenauth\n  processors:\n    transform/route:\n      log_statements:\n        - context: log\n          conditions:\n            - resource.attributes[\"service.name\"] == \"claude-code\"\n          statements:\n            - set(resource.attributes[\"data_stream.dataset\"], \"claude_code\")\n        - context: log\n          conditions:\n            - resource.attributes[\"service.name\"] == \"cowork\"\n          statements:\n            - set(resource.attributes[\"data_stream.dataset\"], \"claude_cowork\")\n  exporters:\n    elasticsearch:\n      endpoints: [\"https://your-elasticsearch:9200\"]\n      user: \"${env:ES_USERNAME}\"\n      password: \"${env:ES_PASSWORD}\"\n  service:\n    extensions: [bearertokenauth]\n    pipelines:\n      logs:\n        receivers: [otlp]\n        processors: [transform/route]\n        exporters: [elasticsearch]\n```\n\n### Option 2: Elastic Cloud Managed OTLP Endpoint (no gateway needed)\n\nIf you are running Elastic Cloud (Serverless or Hosted), you can skip the gateway entirely. Elastic's [Managed OTLP (mOTLP) endpoint](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/opentelemetry/motlp) provides a resilient, auto-scaling ingestion layer that accepts OTLP data directly — no collector infrastructure to deploy or maintain.\n\nTo use it, point Claude Code's OTLP exporter directly at your Elastic Cloud mOTLP endpoint:\n\n```shell\nexport CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_TELEMETRY=1\nexport OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp\nexport OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=otlp\nexport OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL=http/protobuf\nexport OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT=\"https://\u003cyour-motlp-endpoint\u003e\"\nexport OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS=\"Authorization=ApiKey \u003cyour-api-key\u003e\"\nexport OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES=\"data_stream.dataset=claude_code\"\n```\n\nThe `data_stream.dataset` resource attribute is important here; it controls which data stream receives the logs. Without it, data lands in a generic OTel data stream where your custom index templates and ingest pipelines will not apply. Set it to `claude_code` or `claude_cowork` so the data routes to the dedicated `logs-claude_code.otel-*` or `logs-claude_cowork.otel-*` streams with the correct field mappings.\n\nWith mOTLP, you get native OTLP ingestion with automatic data stream routing, a built-in failure store to protect data during indexing issues, and no APM Server requirement.\n\nThe managed endpoint supports all the same custom index templates and ingest pipelines described below, you just don't need to operate the gateway.\n\nFor full setup details, see the [Elastic Cloud Managed OTLP documentation](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/opentelemetry/motlp). Note that it is not currently possible to control index permissions for mOTLP.\n\n## Custom Elasticsearch mappings and ingest pipelines\n\nBy default, OTel attributes are indexed as keywords in Elasticsearch. That works for filtering and grouping, but it breaks numeric aggregations. You cannot SUM or AVG a keyword field. We created custom mappings to fix the field types and an ingest pipeline to parse JSON string fields into structured objects.\n\n### Component template\n\nThe component template overrides the default keyword mappings for numeric and boolean fields, and adds `flattened` type mappings for the JSON-encoded tool parameters:\n\n```json\nPUT _component_template/logs-claude_code.otel@custom\n{\n  \"template\": {\n    \"mappings\": {\n      \"properties\": {\n        \"cost_usd\":                  { \"type\": \"float\" },\n        \"duration_ms\":               { \"type\": \"long\" },\n        \"input_tokens\":              { \"type\": \"long\" },\n        \"output_tokens\":             { \"type\": \"long\" },\n        \"cache_creation_tokens\":     { \"type\": \"long\" },\n        \"cache_read_tokens\":         { \"type\": \"long\" },\n        \"prompt_length\":             { \"type\": \"long\" },\n        \"tool_result_size_bytes\":    { \"type\": \"long\" },\n        \"success\":                   { \"type\": \"boolean\" },\n        \"tool_parameters_flattened\": { \"type\": \"flattened\" },\n        \"tool_input_flattened\":      { \"type\": \"flattened\" }\n      }\n    }\n  }\n}\n```\n\nThe `flattened` type is important here. `tool_parameters` and `tool_input` arrive as JSON strings containing nested keys like `mcp_server_name`, `mcp_tool_name`, `bash_command`, or `command`. By parsing them into `flattened` fields, you can query individual keys without creating an unbounded number of mapped fields.\n\nA future enhancement will be to extract high-value fields from these JSON payloads into dedicated mapped fields — things like MCP server names, tool names, and bash commands — to drive richer analytics, aggregations, and detection rules directly on those values.\n\n### Index template\n\nThe index template composes in all the standard OTel component templates plus our custom one. It matches both `logs-claude_code.otel-*` and `logs-claude_cowork.otel-*` so both data streams share the same field mappings:\n\n```json\nPUT _index_template/logs-claude_code.otel\n{\n  \"index_patterns\": [\n    \"logs-claude_code.otel-*\",\n    \"logs-claude_cowork.otel-*\"\n  ],\n  \"composed_of\": [\n    \"logs@mappings\",\n    \"logs@settings\",\n    \"otel@mappings\",\n    \"otel@settings\",\n    \"logs-otel@mappings\",\n    \"semconv-resource-to-ecs@mappings\",\n    \"logs@custom\",\n    \"logs-otel@custom\",\n    \"logs-claude_code.otel@custom\",\n    \"ecs@mappings\"\n  ],\n  \"priority\": 150,\n  \"data_stream\": {},\n  \"allow_auto_create\": true,\n  \"ignore_missing_component_templates\": [\n    \"logs@custom\",\n    \"logs-otel@custom\"\n  ]\n}\n```\n\n### Ingest pipeline\n\nThe ingest pipeline parses `tool_parameters` and `tool_input` from JSON strings into objects, writing to separate `*_flattened` target fields to avoid conflicts with the original keyword-mapped attributes:\n\n```json\nPUT _ingest/pipeline/logs-claude_code.otel@custom\n{\n  \"description\": \"Parse JSON string fields in Claude Code/Cowork OTel telemetry\",\n  \"processors\": [\n    {\n      \"json\": {\n        \"field\": \"attributes.tool_parameters\",\n        \"target_field\": \"tool_parameters_flattened\",\n        \"if\": \"ctx.attributes?.tool_parameters != null \u0026\u0026 ctx.attributes.tool_parameters.startsWith('{')\",\n        \"ignore_failure\": true\n      }\n    },\n    {\n      \"json\": {\n        \"field\": \"attributes.tool_input\",\n        \"target_field\": \"tool_input_flattened\",\n        \"if\": \"ctx.attributes?.tool_input != null \u0026\u0026 ctx.attributes.tool_input.startsWith('{')\",\n        \"ignore_failure\": true\n      }\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\nAfter creating all three resources, new data flowing into the `logs-claude_code.otel-*` and `logs-claude_cowork.otel-*` data streams will have correct numeric field types and searchable structured tool parameters.\n\n## Configuring telemetry export\n\nClaude Code and Cowork are configured differently. Claude Code uses standard OpenTelemetry environment variables. Cowork OTel export is configured centrally by administrators in the Anthropic admin portal.\n\nClaude Code supports [managed settings](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/settings#settings-files) that are deployed by IT and cannot be overridden by users. The configuration is a JSON file containing an `env` block:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"env\": {\n    \"CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_TELEMETRY\": \"1\",\n    \"OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER\": \"otlp\",\n    \"OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER\": \"otlp\",\n    \"OTEL_LOG_TOOL_DETAILS\": \"1\",\n    \"OTEL_LOG_USER_PROMPTS\": \"1\",\n    \"OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL\": \"http/protobuf\",\n    \"OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT\": \"https://your-otel-gateway:443\",\n    \"OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS\": \"Authorization=Bearer your-token\"\n  }\n}\n```\n\nThis managed settings file can be delivered via MDM (Jamf, Intune), server-managed settings through the Claude.ai Admin Console, or file-based deployment. See the [Claude Code managed settings documentation](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/settings#settings-files) for the full list of delivery mechanisms and their security properties.\n\nFor local testing, you can put the same configuration in `~/.claude/settings.json` on your own machine before rolling it out organization-wide.\n\n### Cowork\n\nCowork OTel export is configured centrally by administrators in the Anthropic admin portal. Administrators set the OTLP endpoint and authentication headers in the admin console, and Cowork instances automatically pick up the configuration. Prompt content and tool details are included by default without requiring additional flags.\n\nBecause Cowork runs in a sandbox, the OTel gateway endpoint must be allowlisted for outbound network access from the sandbox environment. Without this, telemetry export will fail silently.\n\n## Security use cases\n\n![](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltc8890c89905f36a3/6a7d7e105588adeefdee4264/image1.png)\n\nThe combination of event types, identity fields, and tool parameters creates a rich dataset for security operations. Here are the use cases we are building detection and investigation capabilities around.\n\n**Tool invocation auditing.** Every tool call is logged with the tool name and input parameters. For MCP tools, this includes the MCP server name and tool name (e.g., `slack_send_message`, `github/search_issues`). You can detect unauthorized data access, unusual shell commands, or unexpected MCP server interactions. Use the `attributes.tool_name + attributes.tool_parameters` fields.\n\n**Session reconstruction.** The `session.id` field combined with `event.sequence` provides a monotonically increasing counter within each session. You can reconstruct the complete sequence of a Claude session: what the user asked, what tools ran, what data was accessed, and what APIs were called. This is valuable for incident response — if you detect a suspicious tool call, you can pull the full session context.\n\n**Permission decision analysis.** The [`attributes.event.name`](http://attributes.event.name)`: tool_decision` events provide insight into how each tool use was approved. This lets you detect users auto-approving risky tool categories, or identify unusual permission patterns across the fleet.\n\n| Decision Source | Meaning |\n| ----- | ----- |\n| `config` | Auto-allowed by settings or policy |\n| `hook` | Decided by a configured hook script |\n| `user_temporary` | User clicked accept for this invocation |\n| `user_permanent` | User clicked \"always allow\" for this tool |\n| `user_abort` | User aborted the session |\n| `user_reject` | User explicitly rejected the tool use |\n\n**Cost anomaly detection.** The `cost_usd` field on every `api_request` event enables per-request, per-session, and per-user cost tracking. You can alert on unusually expensive sessions or identify users with outsized consumption patterns.\n\n**Correlating with EDR data.** If you are running [Elastic Defend](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/security/elastic-defend) on your endpoints, you can correlate Claude's OTel telemetry with EDR process and file events to understand the full picture. When Claude Code executes a Bash command, the OTel `tool_result` event tells you what the agent decided to run and why (via the preceding `user_prompt`). The corresponding Elastic Defend process event tells you exactly what happened on the host — child processes spawned, files written, network connections made. Joining these two data sources by timestamp and host gives you both the intent (from the AI agent telemetry) and the impact (from endpoint telemetry) in a single investigation.\n\n**MCP server access monitoring.** As organizations connect AI agents to internal systems through MCP, monitoring which servers are accessed and with what tools becomes critical. The `tool_parameters_flattened.mcp_server_name` and `tool_parameters_flattened.mcp_tool_name` fields provide this visibility.\n\nFor example, to see tool invocations for Slack, you could query `tool_name: \"mcp_tool\" AND tool_parameters_flattened.mcp_tool_name:slack*`.  \n\n![](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt9b9e06f5948a32fb/6a7d7e13c2cc09721a2465e9/image3.png)\n\n## Beyond OTel: Claude enterprise audit logs\n\nTelemetry from Claude Code and Cowork covers agent activity on endpoints, but it doesn't capture everything. For full visibility, organizations should also collect [Claude enterprise audit logs](https://support.claude.com/en/articles/9970975-access-audit-logs) from the Compliance API. This is the only source of activity on the web interface (claude.ai) and of traditional security audit events, such as login activity, permission changes, and organization-level administration. Combining both data sources gives security teams a complete picture across all Claude products.\n\n## Conclusion\n\nAI coding assistants and autonomous agents are becoming part of the standard enterprise toolkit. If your security team doesn't have visibility into what these tools are doing, you have a gap. Claude Code and Cowork ship with OpenTelemetry support that provides exactly the kind of telemetry security teams need; identity, session context, tool invocation details, cost data, and permission decisions. Elastic's native OTel ingestion capabilities, whether through the Managed OTLP endpoint on Elastic Cloud or the EDOT Collector in a self-managed environment, make it straightforward to get this data into Elasticsearch, where you can search it, build dashboards, and write detection rules.\n\nIf you want to get started, sign up for a [free trial of Elastic Cloud](https://cloud.elastic.co/registration) and try the Managed OTLP endpoint, or install the [EDOT OTel Collector](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/edot-collector) in your existing environment.\n\n## References\n\n- [Claude Code Monitoring \u0026 Telemetry](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/monitoring-usage)  \n- [Claude Code Settings — Managed A quarterly look at Elastic’s security integrations ecosystem\n\nSecurity teams can only protect what they can see. Gaps in coverage, like a macOS fleet generating logs that never reach your SIEM, an email gateway running in isolation, or a cloud environment producing findings that stay siloed in the vendor console, are easily exploited by attackers.  \n\nElastic’s answer to this is continuous and open investment in third-party integrations, built on the belief that a strong security ecosystem requires deep integrations that make data from every corner of the stack searchable and contextualized. Today, we’re announcing nine new integrations for Elastic Security spanning cloud security, endpoint visibility, email threat detection, identity and SIEM.   \n\nEach integration ships with ingest pipelines that normalize and structure data out of the box, along with prebuilt dashboards that serve as an immediate starting point for visualization and analysis, so teams can search, correlate and investigate across new data sources from day one without writing or maintaining parsers.\n\n## macOS Security Events\n\nElastic Defend, the native integration that delivers Elastic Endpoint Security, collects rich security telemetry on macOS, and it is intentionally focused on high-value detection signals rather than full system auditing. Login and logout events, account creation and deletion, service registration changes and application diagnostic logs all live outside that scope, leaving threat hunters and IR teams without complete macOS context. The macOS Security Events integration complements Elastic Defend, providing the same depth of OS-level visibility offered to Windows devices via the Windows Event Logs integration.\n\nMacOS endpoints generate tens of thousands of unified log entries per endpoint. Left unfiltered, that volume creates noise rather than signals. This integration ships with predicate-based filters that scope ingestion to security-relevant events: authentication activity, process execution, network connections, file system changes, and system configuration modifications. \n\nThese predicate-based filters enable comprehensive macOS coverage without the cost or complexity of ingesting everything. Once ingested, these events are immediately available to Elastic Security’s AI Assistant. Analysts can ask natural-language questions like \"Show me all privilege escalation attempts on macOS endpoints in the last 24 hours\" or \"Summarize login failures for this host”, turning raw unified log entries into actionable investigation context without writing a single query.\n\nCheck out the [macOS Security Events](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/macos) integration.\n\n## IBM QRadar\n\nFor teams running IBM QRadar in parallel with Elastic Security, alert ingestion into Elastic has become easier. The QRadar integration collects offense records from QRadar’s offense and rules endpoints, enriching each alert with the triggering rule’s name, ID, type and ownership, so analysts can triage in Elastic without switching back to QRadar. \n\nThis integration is the foundation of Elastic’s SIEM migration workflow for QRadar, which mirrors the capability already available for [Splunk](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/splunk). Teams can also use [Automatic Migration](https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/from-qradar-to-elastic) for migrating their QRadar rules into Elastic. It uses semantic search and generative AI to map existing rules to Elastic’s 1,300+ prebuilt detections, and translates anything that doesn’t map directly into ES|QL, allowing you to consolidate your SIEM footprint without manually rebuilding your entire detection library. \n\nCheck out the [IBM QRadar](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/ibm_qradar) integration.\n\n## Proofpoint Essentials\n\nFor Enterprise customers, Proofpoint’s TAP (Targeted Attack Protection) has been available in Elastic. To provide the same email threat visibility to SMB environments and the MSP and MSSPs who serve them, Proofpoint Essentials is now available.\n\nThe Proofpoint Essentials integration streams four event types into Elastic Security: \n\n* Clicks on malicious URLs that were blocked  \n* Clicks that were permitted  \n* Messages blocked for containing threats recognized by URL Defense or Attachment Defense  \n* Messages delivered despite containing those threats\n\nTo easily surface this data, two prebuilt dashboards are available:\n\n![Clicks Overview dashboard shows blocked versus permitted click trends over time, broken down by threat status and classification.](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt718b2adde02b169d/6a7d7f8e77b03468723fc623/image2.png \"Clicks Overview dashboard shows blocked versus permitted click trends over time, broken down by threat status and classification.\")  \n\n![Threat Overview dashboard shows blocked versus permitted click trends over time, broken down by threat status and classification.](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blte8f22253ce0da453/6a7d7f90dd26d2b71d2a7245/image1.png \"Threat Overview dashboard shows blocked versus permitted click trends over time, broken down by threat status and classification.\")  \n\nFor an SMB SOC team, this means phishing attempts, malware detections and policy violations land in the same platform as the rest of your security telemetry, removing the need to switch platforms to understand the full context of a threat. \n\nCheck out the [Proofpoint Essentials](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/proofpoint_essentials) integration.\n\n## AWS Security Hub\n\nAWS Security Hub aggregates findings across your AWS environment, but investigating those findings means staying inside the AWS console, separate from the rest of your team’s security data. The Elastic integration changes this by pulling Security Hub findings into Elastic in Open Cybersecurity Schema Framework (OCSF) format and normalizing them to ECS, offering schema-consistent data that’s immediately searchable via ES|QL.\n\nFindings land in the [Elastic Vulnerability Findings](https://www.elastic.co/docs/solutions/security/cloud/findings-page-3) page, integrating AWS cloud security posture directly into the workflows already in place. From there, you can correlate Security Hub data with signals from other sources \\- endpoint alerts, identity events, network telemetry \\- to build a fuller picture of risk across your AWS environment and investigate faster than the native console allows.\n\nCheck out the [AWS Security Hub](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/aws_securityhub) integration.\n\n## More new Elastic Security integrations\n\nIn addition to the featured integrations above, the following integrations are now available, each shipping with prebuilt dashboards for immediate value:\n\n* [JupiterOne](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/jupiter_one): Asset intelligence and cloud attack surface monitoring, ingesting cross-tool alerts, CVE findings, and threat detections enriched with MITRE ATT\\\u0026CK mappings and CVSS scores, and host context for unified risk visibility.  \n* [Airlock Digital](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/airlock_digital): Application allowlisting and execution control telemetry, capturing blocked process executions with command lines, file hashes and publisher context, so unauthorized execution attempts are visible and correlatable alongside the rest of your endpoint detections.   \n* [Island Browser](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/island_browser): Enterprise browser security events spanning user navigation, device posture, compromised credential detection and admin activity, extending Elastic’s visibility to BYOD and unmanaged devices where traditional endpoint agents can’t be deployed.  \n* [Ironscales](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/ironscales): AI-powered phishing detection events capturing email metadata, sender reputation, affected mailbox counts and suspicious links, correlatable with endpoint and identity data for faster investigation and response.   \n* [Cyera](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/cyera): Data security posture management events, surfacing sensitive data risks including exposure severity, affected record counts, compliance framework violations, and datastore ownership across cloud environments, so sensitive data exposure doesn’t stay siloed in a separate DSPM console. \n\n## Get started\n\nThese integrations Elastic’s open approach to security. All nine integrations in this roundup ship with prebuilt dashboards and native ECS mappings, giving your team immediate visibility with no additional setup or custom visualization work required.\n\nFrom there, findings, alerts and logs are immediately available to Elastic’s broader [detection and investigation capabilities](https://www.elastic.co/docs/solutions/security/ai/identify-investigate-document-threats): Attack Discovery for surfacing multi-stage threats, AI Assistant for natural-language investigation and guided response, and to ES|QL and EQL for custom detection and hunting queries.\n\n- [Browse available integrations](https://www.elastic.co/integrations/data-integrations?solution=security)  \n- [Learn about migrating to Elastic Security from other SIEMs](https://www.elastic.co/blog/automatic-migration-ai-rule-translation)\n\nHave questions or feedback? Join \\#security-siem in the [Elastic Stack Community Slack](https://www.elastic.co/community/)."},"seo":{"seo_title_l10n":null,"seo_description_l10n":null,"do_not_index":false,"nofollow":false},"summary_l10n":"Elastic Security Labs announces nine new \u0026 Tools","title_l10n":"Integrations \u0026 Tools","url":"/security-labs/blog/category/integrations-tools","locale":"en-us","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","updated_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","created_at":"2026-08-12T12:28:20.823Z","updated_at":"2026-08-12T12:28:20.823Z","ACL":{},"_version":1,"coding_language":false,"tags":[],"_in_progress":false,"publish_details":{"time":"2026-08-26T17:21:49.330Z","user":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","environment":"blt835e2c3b7e9eb7fb","locale":"en-us"}}],"created_at":"2026-08-13T08:41:17.571Z","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","date":"2026-03-13T00:00:00.000Z","main_content":{"markdown_l10n":"At the core of Elastic Security lie [outstanding detection capabilities](https://www.elastic.co/blog/elastic-security-detection-engineering), allowing users to [create](https://www.elastic.co/blog/elastic-security-building-effective-threat-hunting-detection-rules), test, tune, manage, deploy detection rules, as code, in their environments. The ability to create robust detections is critical for Security Operations as detection logic elevates threat signal from the telemetry noise.\n\nThis article highlights how Elastic's new Terraform resources for security detection rules and exceptions expand practitioners' capabilities for detection-as-code deployment. Below you will find examples of defining and deploying your detection artifacts in Elastic Security with Terraform. We will also show how you can use Elastic's AI Agent to help quickly create the Terraform configuration for your custom rules. Finally, it also provides guidance on when to use the Elastic Stack Terraform [provider](https://registry.terraform.io/providers/elastic/elasticstack/latest/docs/resources/kibana_security_detection_rule) versus [tools from the detection-rules repository](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/README.md#detections-as-code-dac).\n\n## Managing Elastic with Terraform\n\n[Terraform is](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform) a tool created by HashiCorp (now IBM) to manage infrastructure in the cloud, or in self-managed environments, as code. With a simple stroke of HCL (HashiCorp configuration language), users can define the desired state of their cloud provider infrastructure, application, configuration, and in Elastic’s case, cluster settings, configuration, indices or streams, and now also detection rules and alerts, as fully configurable, traceable, and reviewable code in your favorite source management tool.\n\nThe [Elastic Stack Terraform provider](https://registry.terraform.io/providers/elastic/elasticstack/latest/docs/resources/kibana_security_detection_rule) helps search, observability, and security professionals, as well as DevOps and SREs, configure their Elastic clusters with the right indices and mappings for their search use cases, SLOs or Fleet policies for their observability use case, and now, detection rules, alerts, and exceptions for their security use case. It can easily configure those, and many more objects and settings in the Elastic Stack. \n\n## Security Detection rules - now as code with Terraform\n\nWith [V0.12.0](https://github.com/elastic/terraform-provider-elasticstack/releases/tag/v0.12.0) and [V0.13.0](https://github.com/elastic/terraform-provider-elasticstack/releases/tag/v0.13.0) of the [Elastic Stack Terraform provider,](https://registry.terraform.io/providers/elastic/elasticstack/latest/docs/resources/kibana_security_detection_rule) users can now manage their Detection rules and rule exceptions using Terraform. This is especially useful for users who have already been managing their Elastic deployments with Terraform and want to extend it to Detection rules.\n\n### Using the Elastic Stack Terraform Provider to deploy Rules and Exceptions\n\nLet's look at an example of using the Elastic Stack Terraform Provider to deploy an Elastic Security Rule. In this example, we want to detect Windows Service Accounts that are performing an interactive logon on a host. \n\nService accounts typically have elevated privileges and rarely-rotated passwords, making them high-value targets for attackers. Since these accounts should only perform automated service logons, an interactive logon can indicate credential theft or misuse.\n\nThe first thing we need to think of is what telemetry we need to see which logons are happening on our host. [Logon events](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/previous-versions/windows/it-pro/windows-10/security/threat-protection/auditing/event-4624) are logged by the Windows Local Security Authority Subsystem Service (LSASS) whenever a logon session is successfully created on the machine. We can pick this up via an Elastic Agent with the [Windows Integration](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/windows) installed. \n\nThe data will be written by the Elastic Agent into the system.security Data Stream, we can match it with this index pattern: `logs-system.security-*.` We also know that Logon events generate event code `4624` and that, in our example, the service account name starts with `svc` or ends with `$`.  In addition, an interactive login will have a logon type of `interactive`.\n\nSo, we can match these events with an [ES|QL](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/query-languages/esql) rule like: \n\n```sql\nFROM logs-system.security-\\*  \n| WHERE event.code \\== \"4624\" AND (user.name LIKE \"svc\\_\\*\" OR user.name LIKE \"svc-\\*\"  \n     OR user.name LIKE \"\\*\\_svc\" OR user.name LIKE \"\\*$\")  \n     AND winlog.logon.type IN (\"Interactive\", \"RemoteInteractive\",  \n         \"CachedInteractive\", \"CachedRemoteInteractive\")  \n```\n\nThere may be situations where we don't want this rule to run, for example, if there is a legacy application that we want to permit interactive logons from. So, we can create an [Exception Item](https://www.elastic.co/docs/solutions/security/detect-and-alert/rule-exceptions), like: `user.name IS svc\\_sqlbackup`. \n\nNow that we know what we want the Rule and its Exceptions to look like, we can use the Terraform provider's [elasticstack\\_kibana\\_security\\_detection\\_rule](https://registry.terraform.io/providers/elastic/elasticstack/latest/docs/resources/kibana_security_detection_rule), [elasticstack\\_kibana\\_security\\_exception\\_list](https://registry.terraform.io/providers/elastic/elasticstack/latest/docs/resources/kibana_security_exception_list), and [elasticstack\\_kibana\\_security\\_exception\\_item](https://registry.terraform.io/providers/elastic/elasticstack/latest/docs/resources/kibana_security_exception_item) resources to define them in code. \n\nTurning ES|QL rules into Terraform's configuration syntax, [HCL](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/syntax/configuration), is a great use case for Elastic's [AI Agent](https://www.elastic.co/docs/solutions/security/ai/agent-builder/agent-builder).   \nElastic AI Agent capabilities help accelerate security operations across a wide range of tasks \\- from [alerts triage and incident response](https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/speeding-apt-attack-discovery-confirmation-with-attack-discovery-workflows-and-agent-builder) to helping with detection lifecycle tasks. \n\nSimply open AI Agent, and ask it to create Terraform configurations based on your query and exceptions. \n\n![](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blte5ea7e698a14d612/6a7d831ebd219851d67552f9/image2.png)\n\nYou should end up with something like this: \n\n![](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltca7adc2a6c26184c/6a7d8322c2e91402ef013cd5/image1.png)\n\nHere's a closer look at the code.\n\nThere are a few elements to call out specifically:\n- `type`: The type of exception list. For example: detection, endpoint, or endpoint_trusted_apps\n- `namespace_type`: Determines whether the exception list is available in all Kibana spaces or just the single space in which it was created.\n\n```\nresource \"elasticstack_kibana_security_exception_list\" \"svc_account_interactive_login\" {\n  list_id        = \"svc-account-interactive-login-exceptions\"\n  name           = \"Service Account Interactive Login Exceptions\"\n  description    = \"Documented exceptions for service accounts that legitimately require interactive logon\"\n  type           = \"detection\"\n  namespace_type = \"single\"\n  tags           = [\"service-accounts\",\"windows\",\"authentication\"]\n}  \n```\n\nThis creates a new exception list.\n\nOf note, the `entries` array contains the conditions under which the exception applies.\n\n```\nresource \"elasticstack_kibana_security_exception_item\" \"svc_sqlbackup\" {\n  list_id        = elasticstack_kibana_security_exception_list.svc_account_interactive_login.list_id\n  item_id        = \"svc-sqlbackup-exception\"\n  name           = \"svc_sqlbackup - Legacy SQL Backup Agent\"\n  description    = \"Approved exception: Legacy SQL backup agent requires interactive logon per vendor documentation.\"\n  type           = \"simple\"\n  namespace_type = \"single\"\n  tags           = [\"sql\",\"backup\",\"approved\"]\nentries = [\n    {\n      field    = \"user.name\"\n      type     = \"match\"\n      operator = \"included\"\n      value    = \"svc_sqlbackup\"\n    }\n  ]\n} \n```\n\nThis adds our exception: that we don't want the rule to run if the username is `svc\\_sqlbackup`.\n\nOf note, the elements from `enabled` to the `technique` array are examples of the other properties that can be set on a rule.\n\n```\nresource \"elasticstack_kibana_security_detection_rule\" \"svc_account_interactive_login\" {\n  name        = \"Service Account Interactive Login\"\n  description = \u003c\u003c-EOT\n    Detects interactive logins by service accounts. Service accounts should authenticate\n    via service (Type 5) or batch (Type 4) logon types, not interactively. Interactive\n    logins by service accounts may indicate credential theft or misuse.\n\n    This rule identifies service accounts by common naming conventions (svc_*, svc-*,\n    *_svc) and managed service accounts (*$).\n  EOT\n\n  type     = \"esql\"\n  language = \"esql\"\n  query    = \u003c\u003c-EOT\n    FROM logs-system.security-* metadata _id, _version, _index\n    | WHERE event.code == \"4624\"\n      AND (user.name LIKE \"svc_*\" OR user.name LIKE \"svc-*\" OR user.name LIKE \"*_svc\" OR user.name LIKE \"*$\")\n      AND winlog.logon.type IN (\"Interactive\", \"RemoteInteractive\", \"CachedInteractive\", \"CachedRemoteInteractive\")\n    | KEEP @timestamp, host.name, user.name, user.domain, winlog.logon.type, source.ip, _id, _version, _index\n  EOT\n\n  enabled    = true \n  severity   = \"high\"\n  risk_score = 73\n\n  from     = \"now-6m\"\n  to       = \"now\"\n  interval = \"5m\"\n\n  author  = [\"Security Team\"]\n  license = \"Elastic License v2\"\n  tags    = [\n    \"Domain: Endpoint\",\n    \"OS: Windows\",\n    \"Use Case: Identity and Access Audit\",\n    \"Tactic: Initial Access\",\n    \"Data Source: Windows Security Event Log\"\n  ]\n\n  false_positives = [\n    \"Service accounts with documented exceptions that require interactive logon\",\n    \"Break-glass procedures during incident response\",\n    \"Initial service account configuration or troubleshooting\"\n  ]\n\n  references = [\n    \"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/architecture/service-accounts-on-premises\",\n    \"https://blog.quest.com/10-microsoft-service-account-best-practices/\",\n    \"https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1078/002/\"\n  ]\n\n  threat = [\n    {\n      framework = \"MITRE ATT\u0026CK\"\n      tactic = {\n        id        = \"TA0001\"\n        name      = \"Initial Access\"\n        reference = \"https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0001/\"\n      }\n      technique = [\n        {\n          id        = \"T1078\"\n          name      = \"Valid Accounts\"\n          reference = \"https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1078/\"\n          subtechnique = [\n            {\n              id        = \"T1078.002\"\n              name      = \"Domain Accounts\"\n              reference = \"https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1078/002/\"\n            }\n          ]\n        }\n      ]\n    }\n  ]\n\n  exceptions_list = [\n    {\n      id             = elasticstack_kibana_security_exception_list.svc_account_interactive_login.id\n      list_id        = elasticstack_kibana_security_exception_list.svc_account_interactive_login.list_id\n      namespace_type = elasticstack_kibana_security_exception_list.svc_account_interactive_login.namespace_type\n      type           = elasticstack_kibana_security_exception_list.svc_account_interactive_login.type\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\nFinally, we define the rule, including the ES|QL query we provided earlier and MITRE ATT\\\u0026CK classification.\n\nYou can add these resource definitions into one configuration file (perhaps security-rules.tf), add it to your [configured](https://registry.terraform.io/providers/elastic/elasticstack/latest/docs#kibana) Elastic Stack terraform directory, and then run the \\`terraform apply\\` command and parameter to deploy the Rule.\n\n```shell\nterraform apply --auto-approve\n```\n\nSince `terraform apply` runs a plan before making changes, it will automatically detect if anyone has edited a rule directly in Kibana and show you exactly what drifted: no manual exports or diffs needed.\n\nAfter Terraform has made the changes, we can see the Rule in Kibana: \n\n![](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blte66ac99b0a40cb26/6a7d8325bdcff04720c400a2/image5.png)\n\nWe can also see the Exception List: \n\n![](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltb87fca154e808bcf/6a7d832805b7b530b9188b7e/image4.png)\n\nThis way, you can define your detections in Terraform and benefit from automatic deployment along with other objects you manage with Terraform.\n\n## Terraform workspaces for multi-space Elastic deployments\n\nTerraform uses a concept called “[workspaces](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/state/workspaces)” allowing you to reuse the same infrastructure code for multiple deployments, for example, a dev, testing, and production environment. This concept is useful for managing rules across multiple deployments and/or Kibana spaces.\n\n## Managing detections with Terraform and Detections as code\n\nElastic also has [Detections as Code functionality](https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/detection-as-code-timeline-and-new-features) available via our open [detection-rules repository.](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules) \n\nThe two tools have complementary strengths and are aligned with different user profiles and workflow stages for implementing Detections as Code.\n\n### Detection as Code features in detection-rules\n\n* **Best fit user profile**: Detection engineers  \n* **Intended workflow phase**: Rule authoring and validation\n\nWith dual-sync between your GitHub repo and Kibana, linting, schema validation, and unit-testing, detection-rules functionality is well-suited to experienced Detection Engineers comfortable with Git-based version control.\n\n### Elastic Stack Terraform Provider\n\n* **Best fit user profile**: DevOps engineers / Platform teams  \n* **Intended workflow phase**: Deployment and operations\n\nFor users already using Terraform to manage their Elastic clusters, the Terraform Provider is a great fit, bringing consistency to all \"x-as-code\" operations and familiar state management and parameterization.\n\nThe key differences and optimal use cases for each tool are detailed in the comparison table below:\n\n| Workflow Stage | detection-rules | Terraform Provider | Best Fit |\n| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |\n| **Rule Authoring** | Purpose-built tooling: create-rule wizard, TOML schema, KQL/EQL validation, field checks against ECS, Kibana-to-code export. | Standard HCL definitions; teams integrate their preferred validation tooling into existing pipelines. | **detection-rules:** Detection engineers authoring and refining rules daily. Teams wanting to automatically convert rules from Kibana into code. **Terraform:** Teams already using Terraform in their workflows, or teams wanting to automate and deploy detection rules as code, but without an established CI/CD platform. |\n| **Testing \u0026 Validation** | Built-in unit testing framework, schema validation, query validation, configurable test suites. | Terraform tests for optional unit testing. No built-in query validation: the provider relies on the Kibana API to accept or reject rule definitions at apply time. | **detection-rules:** Teams wanting out-of-the-box detection testing. **Terraform:** Platform teams managing rules as part of broader IaC with existing validation pipelines. Teams happy to write custom tests in Terraform. |\n| **Exception Management** | Native exception list handling; export/import with rules, TOML storage, and rule linking. | Exception lists can be referenced via rule attributes. | **detection-rules:** Teams managing exceptions as part of detection content. **Terraform:** Teams managing exceptions as separate infrastructure resources. |\n| **Governance \u0026 Drift Management** | VCS-based with dual sync: push rules from repo to Kibana and export from Kibana back to repo, allowing either to serve as the source of truth. Drift detection is achievable with custom export-and-diff tooling. | VCS-authoritative: state file enforces declared configuration.  Native drift detection: Terraform plan surfaces any out-of-band changes made in Kibana.  | **detection-rules:** Teams comfortable with Git-based workflows and flexible sync models. **Terraform:** Organisations requiring formal state reconciliation and audit trails. |\n| **Rollback** | Git history provides version control; re-import previous versions from the repo. | Revert HCL configuration in Git and re-apply to restore the previous state. | **detection-rules:** Teams using Git-centric recovery workflows. **Terraform:** Organisations with standardised rollback mechanisms across infrastructure and rulesets. |\n| **Parameterisation \u0026 Templating** | Achievable with external preprocessing (Jinja2, etc.) before import. | Native HCL features: variables, locals, for\\_each, dynamic blocks, and modules. | **detection-rules:** Teams not requiring parameterisation or with existing templating solutions.  **Terraform:** Teams wanting native IaC parameterisation. |\n| **Operational Integration** | Focused tooling optimised for detection engineering workflows. | Unified control plane managing detection rules alongside cloud infrastructure, network policies, and other security tooling.  Integrates with other resources that may be required by detections such as external connectors. | **detection-rules:** Specialist detection teams. More flexible if dual-sync (Kibana and repo are both sources of truth).  **Terraform:** Platform teams managing Elastic as part of broader infrastructure. |\n\nIn short, Detection Engineers are better served by the specialized creation and testing tools provided in the `detection-rules` repository, while DevOps/Platform Teams should use the Terraform provider to manage detection rules as part of their broader infrastructure-as-code strategy for deployment and operations.\n\n## Try it out\n\nTo experience the full benefits of what Elastic has to offer for detection engineers, upgrade to 9.3 or start your Elastic Security [free trial](https://cloud.elastic.co/registration). Visit [elastic.co/security](https://www.elastic.co/security) to learn more and get started.\n\n*The release and timing of any features or functionality described in this post remain at Elastic's sole discretion. Any features or functionality not currently available may not be delivered on time or at
